Working notes

The project’s own files, as they stand: how the run is organised, where it has got to, the brief each translator reads, the pilot’s lessons, and the log of every ruling. For how the psalter is made, read the method first.

The project The decisions log Handoff Progress The translator’s brief Ps 4 retrospective Collation Vós ou tu

❦

The project

A Brazilian Portuguese translation of the Gallican Psalter meant to be prayed, for a translation of the Breviarium Romanum (1961/62 books). Built by frontier LLMs working through agentic harnesses, with human philological sign-off. Output is public domain.

design.md is the founding design doc: goal, corpus, the style standard ("simple nobility"), a proposed pipeline, the pilot. Read it for the intent. Its pipeline, data model and tooling are a proposal, not a contract — this folder is deliberately empty so whoever does the work can find the shape that actually produces a good psalter. Make folders, formats and scripts as you need them, and say why in this README as you go.

Status (2026-09-20): pilot Latin collated — no wording divergence from the Clementine (collation/report.md). Ps 4 has been through every step the design doc proposes; draft 2 is prayable at ps004/sheet.md, and ps004/retrospective.md says which steps earned their keep and what the slimmer method is. The pilot is Sunday Prime and Compline.

Review surface: the generated site — python3.13 research/psalterium/site.py, then serve research/psalterium/ (python3.13 -m http.server 8765 --bind 127.0.0.1 --directory research/psalterium) and open /site/index.html: a page per psalm (facing columns, popover decisions, the layers Latin → literal → each draft, the audit trail, other psalters beside), the glossary, word studies, method and every open decision in one list. It grew out of the hand-built review.html, which is kept as it was and still shows Ps 4 at draft 2. What follows describes that first page: — Ps 4 in facing columns, the vós/tu pairs and every open decision on one page; choosing an alternative rewrites the psalm so it can be prayed as chosen, and "Copy my decisions" hands the result back as markdown. It is hand-built from ps004/prayed.json draft 2 and vos-tu.md, not generated; rebuild it if either changes. Touching a dotted word opens its decision in place. The "beside it" switch sets other psalters under each verse (Matos Soares 1932 and 1956, Ave Maria, CNBB; Douay-Rheims, RSV-CE, KJV) — built by compare.py into the gitignored consult/compare-ps004.js, because most of them are in copyright; without that file the switch hides itself. Only the Douay-Rheims and Matos Soares 1932 are from the Vulgate (his 1956 edition follows the Hebrew). The 1932 text comes from the text layer of ~/Downloads/Vulgata-Padre-Matos-Soares-1.pdf — pdftotext -f <page> -l <page> is cheap, so never read its pages as images; Ps 4 is PDF pages 1021–1022, and it is the nearest Portuguese antecedent (see ps004/decisions.md). The Bolls copy of the CNBB lacks verse 9.

Waiting on a human: Gustavo's review of the overnight rulings in DECISIONS.md (2026-09-20: he delegated decisions on condition that they be deliberated and documented) — first D2, the rule for how far fluency may pull against the Latin, and D5, confitéri → dar graças a. The machine, meanwhile: the psalter psalm by psalm through sub-agents, 40 minutes apart in batches of two — PROGRESS.md says where it stands, HANDOFF.md how it is run.

How a psalm is made — the method, written for someone who does not know the project, with its drawing — is method.md, the site's Method page. This file and the others on the Working notes page are for whoever works on it.

What is here

  • collation/ — collate.py diffs the DO pilot Latin against the Clementine Vulgate Project text in collation/witness/ (public domain, committed so the diff reproduces offline); diffs.md is its output, report.md the reading of it. Exists because the base text had to be settled before a word was translated. normalise() in the script is the orthography fold a lemmatiser will need later.
  • vos-tu.md — fifteen pilot verses rendered both ways, made so the address could be heard before it was fixed. Decided: vós (2026-09-20); kept as the record of what was weighed.
  • latin.py — reading the DO psalter: orthography fold, prayed-verse lines, cola, pointing skeleton, and the verse as the app points it. Everything else imports it.
  • lemmas.py → lemma-freq.json — LatinCy lemma counts over Pss 1–150, for the rare-word flag. lexicon.py — Lewis & Short lookups from the consult cache.
  • codex.py — runs one Codex role read-only in a folder and stores the reply verbatim with prompt and target hashes. checks.py — the deterministic checks (marks, verse ids, colon length, cadence, rhyme). render.py — flat texts for the critics and the praying sheet.
  • words/ — word studies for the verbs and nouns that will be prayed hundreds of times, because those are decided once for the whole psalter. words.py <name> <regex> --greek … --pt … generates <name>.concordance.md (every occurrence, its place in the verse, the Greek verb behind it from Rahlfs, the pt-PT antecedent's line); <name>.md is the study written on top of it. A study covers: counts and forms; the sense (Lewis & Short via lexicon.py); the neighbours it must stay distinct from, especially where they stand in parallel in one verse; the constraints vós puts on its forms; living liturgical usage, looked up on the web and marked verified or not; candidates; test verses to pray; what is not known. First: words/exaudire.md — recommends escutar, reversing Ps 4 draft 2's atender.
  • ps004/ — Ps 4 pushed through every step the design doc proposes, each step's output kept apart so its worth can be judged: dossier.json (tier 0), interlinear.md (1), literal.json (2), prayed.json (3), checks.md, critic/ (every model verdict, verbatim), prompts/, blind/ (back-translation, run where the Latin is not), sheet.md (what gets prayed), hard-readings.md.

Where this is going: the finished psalter is meant for Divinum Officium, as its Brazilian Portuguese psalter — hence DO's ids, marks and one-verse-per-line shape throughout. DO's present Portuguese (content/do/horas/Portugues/) is not a witness: parts of it are recent AI-assisted contributions of Gustavo's own, and he says it has many mistakes; it appears in the parallels only as the thing being replaced. DECISIONS.md is the log of what has been ruled, by whom and why — Gustavo's rulings, and Claude's overnight ones awaiting his review.

Tooling

  • Python is python3.13. Scripts that parse Latin need the venv: python3.13 -m venv research/psalterium/.venv, then pip install "spacy>=3.8,<3.9" and the LatinCy wheel https://huggingface.co/latincy/la_core_web_lg/resolve/main/la_core_web_lg-3.9.8-py3-none-any.whl (the unversioned -any- wheel name is rejected by current pip).
  • consult/ is gitignored — a cache of things consulted, not redistributed. To rebuild it: lxx-text_accented.csv and lxx-E-verse.csv from github.com/eliranwong/LXX-Rahlfs-1935 (01_wordlist_unicode/ and its alignment_with_OSSP/); bolls-WLC-19-<psalm>.json from https://bolls.life/get-text/WLC/19/<psalm>/; ls_<LETTER>.json from github.com/IohannesArnold/lewis-short-json; lagarde-iuxta-hebraeos_djvu.txt from archive.org/download/psalteriumiuxta00lagagoog/. Bolls' "LXX" is lemmas only — useless as a text.
  • The books consulted are kept in consult/books/ so that they can be consulted again: matos-soares-1932-vulgata-vol1.pdf (Matos Soares' Vulgate translation, 1932; has a text layer — the Psalms are PDF pp. 1015–1300, extracted to consult/ms1932-salmos.txt with pdftotext -f 1015 -l 1300 -layout), diurnal-monastico-1962.pdf (the scan of the Diurnal Monástico, Mosteiro de São Bento do Rio de Janeiro, 1962, Latin–Portuguese) and diurnal-monastico-1962-ocr/ (its OCR, a markdown.md and page folders for each half; the two markdown.md files are also copied flat as consult/diurnal-1.md and diurnal-2.md, which is what diurnal.py reads). hetzenauer-1914/ holds the page scans read for the collation (pp. 487, 535, 557–558, 1009, from sacredbible.org/hetzenauer1914) and the two index pages that map verse ranges to pages. tests/ (tracked) holds the stub-DOM verifiers for review.html and the site, and diurnal-survey.py. Nothing worth keeping lives in /tmp. Both books came from Gustavo's ~/Downloads. Both are in copyright, so nothing of them leaves consult/. consult/ exists only in this working tree — it is gitignored, so deleting the worktree deletes it; copy it out before that.
  • diurnal.py <psalm> pulls one psalm's Portuguese out of the Diurnal OCR into consult/diurnal/ps<NNN>.json, keyed by DO's prayed-verse ids (it recognises the book's Latin column by its words and pairs the Portuguese paragraphs with it). The Diurnal has 78 psalms (the day hours of the monastic office; none of the psalms proper to Matins). Its psalms are Dom Marcos Barbosa's, made "baseada no texto original" (its preface) — from the Hebrew, addressed as Tu — so it is a witness for prayed Brazilian diction and cadence, never for sense. It feeds parallels.py and the comparison table.
  • Bolls files the Douay-Rheims under a chapter count of its own: two stray one-verse chapters (10, a copy of 9:22; 119, "Aleph"), the second half of Ps 9 as chapter 11, and no chapter at all for Vulgate Ps 10 — its text is in ps010/drb.txt, fetched from drbo.org. So Ps 9 is chapters 9 + 11, Vulgate 11–117 are n+1, 118–150 are n+2, and it runs to 152 (parallels.py: drbChapters, drbByHand). The Ps 10 agent found the Ps 9–10 error on 2026-09-21, before either psalm was translated.
  • Codex CLI ≥ 0.155 (older versions cannot run the configured model).

What does not bend

  • Translate the Latin as Latin. LXX and Hebrew explain Jerome; they never correct him.
  • It is a text for the mouth: recited daily, chanted to a psalm tone, facing the Latin column. Cola follow the Latin's flex, mediant and final; prayed verses are never merged or split.
  • What repeats in the Latin repeats identically in the Portuguese — refrains, formulas, doublet psalms.
  • No rendering is final without a human decision, and every rendering can be traced back to how it was made.

What is already in the repo

Verified against the files, 2026-09-20:

  • The Breviary's Latin psalter is at content/do/horas/Latin/Psalterium/Psalmorum/ — a verbatim mirror of Divinum Officium (pinned commit in content/do/meta.json; never edit it). Psalm1.txt–Psalm150.txt, one prayed verse per line, LXX numbering, already pointed: † flex, * mediant, and ‡ — a second mediant: the app renders DO's noflexa default, which moves the asterisk to the ‡ and drops every † (13 verses; see the collation report). It is the text Ember's breviary renders, so translating it keeps both columns aligned in the app. The pilot psalms match the Clementine word for word; each psalm is read against its Hetzenauer 1914 page before translation (all of Sunday Compline done: Pss 4, 90, 133, Lc 2:29–32).
  • Orthography is liturgical: stress accents, j, æ/œ. Useful for cadence; normalise in memory before any lemmatiser sees it.
  • Verse division is the Breviary's, not the Bible's. 194 verse lines in Pss 1–150 carry a letter suffix (4:2a / 4:2b, 17:36b) where a biblical verse is split into two prayed verses, and 74 inline markers ((20), (26a)) in 37 files mark where a prayed verse runs across a biblical boundary. The prayed verse is the unit.
  • No tituli and no diapsalma — Psalm4.txt opens at 4:2a. The Clementine witness in collation/witness/ has every titulus, so translating them is cheap; but DO's Breviary text does not carry them, so the Latin column will never show them.
  • Canticles are Psalm210.txt–Psalm273.txt in the same folder (Psalm233.txt = Nunc dimittis). The invitatory form of Ps 94 (long verses with antiphon slots) is Psalterium/Invitatorium.txt; Psalm94.txt is the Gallican wording but not a plain Ps 94 — it is the Epiphany Matins form, missing 94:6–7a because the antiphon sung in its $ant slots is that verse. The nine places where the invitatory's wording parts from the Gallican are tabled in the collation report. Antiphons and hour assignments are in Psalterium/Psalmi/*.txt.
  • Pilot psalms per that data: Sunday Prime 117, 118(1-16), 118(17-32) (the [Tridentinum] scheme prefixes 53); Sunday Compline 4, 90, 133.
  • Quoniam in saeculum misericordia eius is the refrain of Pss 105, 106, 117. Pss 135 and 99 read in aeternum — a different formula; whether they share a rendering is a choice to make on purpose.
  • A Portuguese antecedent already aligned to the same cola sits at content/do/horas/Portugues/Psalterium/Psalmorum/ — European Portuguese (protecção), so a reference and a contrast, not a base.

Open questions

  • Tituli: in or out of the MVP. A source exists now; the question is only whether an unprayed line belongs in a prayed text.
  • What ‡ shows as upstream with flexes switched on, and what =, ^, _ mean in Invitatorium.txt.
  • Latin against the ear. On Ps 4 the Latinist critic and the judges passed the draft; the stylist called 8 of 10 verses "not native", and most of its fixes leave the Latin (a supplied verb, the Hebrew's sense, a lost repetition). The rule "translate the Latin as Latin" and the priority "it works in the mouth" pull apart here, and only a praying human can say where the line is.
  • Whether a phrase like in idípsum (six places) must have one rendering everywhere, as refrains do.
  • The dossier quotes Rahlfs' LXX from a CCAT-derived repository. Fine for working files; before anything is published as CC0, either confirm that is clean or switch the Greek to Swete.
  • A better iuxta Hebraeos source than the Lagarde OCR, which needs hand repair verse by verse.

Closed: God is addressed as vós throughout the corpus (Gustavo, 2026-09-20) — the register Brazilian Catholics already pray in, and it keeps God and a singular human addressee apart where a psalm switches between them (Ps 90:9). Its known costs are to be managed, not reopened: under vós the imperative of an -ir verb is also the first-person past (ouvi), so such verbs are avoided at imperatives (exaudire → atender); vosso is a syllable longer than teu; God and a plural crowd share a form (Ps 4). The nearest antecedents (Matos Soares 1932, the CNBB Bible) use tu, so vós wording cannot simply be borrowed from them.

Closed: the DO mirror is the working text, verbatim; Hetzenauer 1914 (page scans at sacredbible.org) is the printed authority it is read against, psalm by psalm (2026-09-20).

  • Who the Latinist reviewer is; whether the translation table is published for public comment.
❦

The decisions log

Gustavo, night of 2026-09-20: "as long as you document the decisions, and deliberate thoughtfully on them, you can make decisions and then let me review them in the morning."

So this file is the log of what was decided, by whom, and why. Every ruling by Claude is provisional until Gustavo has read it — each says what it would cost to reverse. Decisions about a single verse live in that psalm's prayed.json (decisions[]: option 0 is the ruling, the other options are what was weighed, why/note/audit give the reasons) and are reviewed on the psalm's page of the site; this file holds what reaches across psalms, and points to the rest. Newest at the bottom. To overturn one: say so, or pick another option on the site and hand back "Copy my decisions".

Status words: Gustavo = his ruling, closed. Claude, for review = ruled overnight under the delegation above. deferred = deliberately not ruled yet, with the reason.

D1 · God is addressed as vós — Gustavo, 2026-09-20

Closed. Record in vos-tu.md and README.md. Consequence carried everywhere: no vós imperative that equals a first-person past (ouvi, abri, parti).

D2 · How far the ear may pull against the Latin — Claude, for review

Question. Ps 4's stylist found eight of ten verses "not native"; the Latinist and the judges found nothing to fix. Gustavo: "I really liked the stylist suggestions, they are really great." Which wins, and by what rule, so that 150 psalms are decided the same way?

Ruling. The working rule of the night becomes the rule: keep the Latin's words, images, repetitions and ambiguities; yield to the ear on grammar and order. A copula, auxiliary or subject pronoun Portuguese needs may be supplied; natural Portuguese order beats the Latin's; of two faithful words the plainer wins. A stylist's fix is taken whenever it changes only how the thing is said. It is refused — and kept as an option — when it swaps the Latin's word for another word's meaning, dissolves a repetition the Latin has, closes an ambiguity the Latin leaves open, or says what the Hebrew says where the Latin says something else. ~~Outer bound: no further from the Latin than Matos Soares 1932 went, usually less.~~ (Struck by Gustavo, 2026-09-21 — D28.)

Why. (1) It is the only rule under which the stylist and the Latinist can both be right: on Ps 4 five of the stylist's six proposals are about grammar and order, and only one (fez maravilhas pelo seu santo, 4:4) changes the sense — and that one is exactly the Hebrew reading the project exists not to import. (2) The text is prayed beside the Latin column; a reader crossing columns must find the same words and images on both sides, but does not need the same syntax. (3) It matches what the one Vulgate-family Portuguese antecedent actually did: Matos Soares supplies sereis in 4:3 and keeps fez maravilhoso o seu santo and compungi-vos. (4) Across Pss 90, 133, 53 and the Nunc dimittis tonight the rule produced clean Latinist gates on the final drafts while taking roughly half of the stylist's remarks — it is workable, not just principled.

Cost of reversing. Towards the ear: the refused stylist options are all recorded as options, so it is a matter of picking them. Towards the Latin: the taken fixes are in each psalm's audit with the draft kept (prayed.v1.json), so they can be walked back verse by verse.

D3 · exaudíre → escutar; audíre → ouvir; inténdere → atender — Claude, for review

Ruling. As recommended in words/exaudire.md. One verb for all 74 places.

Why. Exaudíre is a hearing verb, and the Greek behind it (εἰσακούω 50×, ἐπακούω 25×) does not split along any line the Latin marks, so one Portuguese verb is justified. Ouvir is excluded at the commonest form, the imperative, because ouvi is also "I heard" (D1). Atender (Ps 4 draft 2) says "hear and grant" well but is needed for inténde, which stands parallel to exáudi in 16:1, 54:2, 60:2, 140:1 — two Latin verbs would collapse into one. Escutar is free, plain, works in every person and tense met so far (escutai, escutou-me, me escutará, eu o escutarei), and already circulates in Brazilian devotional use of the versicle (seen in unofficial sources; the official wording was not verified). What is lost: the "and grant" overtone of ex-; judged less costly than a collision.

Cost of reversing. One glossary verb; in each psalm it is (or will be) a single multi-slot decision, so it flips per psalm in one touch. Ps 4 moves from atender to escutar in its draft 3 (D8).

D4 · áuribus percípere → dar ouvidos a — Claude, for review

Proposed by the Ps 53 agent for all eight places (5:2, 16:1b, 38:13, 48:2, 53:4, 83:9, 85:6, 142:1 — list from the concordance, words/percipere.concordance.md). Ruled as proposed. It keeps the ears the Latin names (rule: images stay), stays apart from escutar and atender with which it stands in parallel, and is safe at the vós imperative (dai ≠ dei). Cheap to reverse: one glossary row, one decision per psalm.

D5 · confitéri (to God) → dar graças a — Claude, for review

Question. Confitémini Dómino quóniam bonus and every other place the verb is said to God (not yet counted — a concordance is owed: words.py confiteri): louvar, confessar, or dar graças?

Ruling. Dar graças a, as the Ps 53 agent proposed against my own pencilled louvar. Confessar stays for confessing sin (31:5).

Why. (1) Louvar is needed for laudáre / laus, which stand in parallel with confitéri in 34:18 (Confitébor tibi… laudábo te), 108:30 (Confitébor Dómino… laudábo eum) and 105:47 (confiteámur nómini… in laude tua) — verified in the DO text; the same collision argument as D3. (2) Dar graças a keeps the dative the Latin always has (confitébor tibi, nómini tuo). (3) Confessar o Senhor in current Portuguese means to profess faith in him, or is simply opaque; it fails "no archaism needing a footnote". (4) Dai graças ao Senhor, porque ele é bom is sayable four times running and takes the stress where a psalm tone wants it. Known cost: the noun conféssio (95:6, 103:1, 110:3, 148:14a) will not always follow as ação de graças — conféssio et pulchritúdo is not thanksgiving; the noun is left open and decided where it is met. Ps 117 is being translated with this verb now.

Cost of reversing. High in count, low in labour: it is one multi-slot decision per psalm. Worth Gustavo's attention first of all the glossary rulings.

D6 · salutáre and salus both → salvação — Claude, for review

The Latin distinguishes them only as neuter adjective-noun vs. noun, the Greek has σωτήριον / σωτηρία the same way, and Portuguese has no natural pair (salutar is an adjective of health; socorro and auxílio are taken). 90:16 salutáre meum and the Nunc dimittis salutáre tuum are both a … salvação, which also matches what the ear expects at Compline. If a psalm sets the two side by side, that psalm makes it a decision.

D7 · Tituli are out of the first edition — Claude, for review

DO's psalm files have no tituli, Ember's Latin column shows none, and the text is made to be prayed beside that column. Adding them would need a second Latin source collated to the same standard. They can be added later without touching a prayed verse. Free to reverse.

D8 · Ps 4 moves to draft 3 under D2 and D3 — Claude, for review

Ps 4 was held for Gustavo; under the delegation it is now ruled like the others, his review page keeps every alternative. Draft 2 is kept as ps004/prayed.v2.json. Verse by verse:

RulingWhy
exaudívit, exáudi, exáudiet (4:2a, 2b, 4)escutar — escutou-me, escutai, me escutaráD3
úsquequo gravi corde? (4:3)the stylist's: até quando tereis o coração pesado?supplies a verb, nothing else — grammar, D2; Matos Soares supplies one too (sereis)
mirificávit sanctum suum (4:4)draft: fez maravilhoso o seu santo — stylist refusedfez maravilhas pelo is the Hebrew's sense, not the Latin's; Matos Soares 1932 has the draft's wording
